The model input consisted of a tensor of dimension (96, 33), representing 96 time instances (24 hours of measurements recorded every 15 minutes) from 33 pressure sensors.\n# 2. Material and Methods\n\nThe methodology adopted in this work was characterized by a quantitative and predictive approach, with the objective of developing an artificial intelligence model for the detection and localization of leaks in water distribution networks. The study integrated computational hydraulic modeling for the generation of synthetic data, time series preprocessing, and the development of a hybrid deep neural network architecture.\n\nThe empirical object of the research was the virtual distribution network L-Town, made available by the international scientific competition BattLeDIM (2020). This fictitious network consists of 782 demand nodes, 1 reservoir, and 2 supply sources. The network has an extension of 43.16 km, modeled in 905 sections, and includes 3 valves and 1 pump. For system monitoring, pressure data from 33 sensors distributed throughout the network were used.\n\nDue to the scarcity of real labeled data from leaks in operational environments, the generation of a synthetic dataset was chosen. 10,000 leak scenarios were simulated, each lasting 24 hours with a hydraulic and reporting time step configured for 900 seconds (15 minutes), resulting in time series of 96 points per sensor. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n2022, Garoarsson Et Al. - Graph-Based Learning For Leak Detection and Localisation in Water Distribution Systems | PDF | Computing | Machine Learning (https://www.scribd.com/document/1034059478/2022-Garoarsson-et-al-Graph-based-learning-for-leak-detection-and-localisation-in-water-distribution-systems)\nciteturn27search1 [wordlim: 200] Published: 4.8 years ago; Crawled: 5 months ago; Comparing Networks ... normalised to the interval [0, 1], but operating pressure in L-Town is normally between ∼ 20 − 70 m. ... The methodology was validated on the **BattLeDIM [124]** dataset from the L-Town network.\n\nx_G^(t−T:t−1) = [x_G^(t−T), x_G^(t−T+1), ..., x_G^(t−1)],\n\nto predict the pressures at the next time step ŷ_p^(t).\n\nThe residual signal is defined as the difference between the predicted and reconstructed values:\n\nr(t) = ŷ_p^(t) − ŷ_r^(t).\n\nUnder normal (non-leak) conditions, the residual signal remains stationary, influenced only by random noise. A deviation from this behavior indicates the presence of a leak. The residual signal on edges to localize the leak is defined as:\n\nr_uv(t) = r_v(t) − r_u(t),\n\nwhere u and v are the nodes connected by an edge, a leakage is detected when the moving average of the residual, \\bar{r}_uv(t) = 1/m ∑_{i=0}^{m−1} r_uv(t − i), exceeds a threshold \\bar{ρ}_uv(t) = α \\bar{σ}_uv(t), where α controls the rate of false alarms and \\bar{σ}_uv(t) is the standard deviation over the window.\n\nTo ensure accurate localization, the authors applied a **Chebyshev spectral graph convolutional network** (Section 4.2.2), which preserves high-frequency (localized) information. For the reconstructor, the input channel size c_in is set to 1, while for the predictor, it is T, corresponding to the time window size.\n\nThe methodology was validated on the **BattLeDIM [124]** dataset from the L-Town network. The EPANET nominal model and sensor measurements from 2018 were used to train the reconstructor and predictor, with documented leak information (size and inflow) as ground truth. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30].\n## 3. Experimental setups\n\n### 3.1. Dataset\n\nReal-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\n\nImage: Fig 2\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\n\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\n\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nMachine Learning in Water Networks Survey | PDF | Machine Learning | Time Series (https://www.scribd.com/document/944068465/2410-12461v1)\nciteturn27search4 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2.0 years ago; Crawled: 11 months ago; Town ... model-based drift explana- L- ... [34] comb ✗ pressure forcast neural network – UKprediction and historical data\n## 5 Approaches for Leakage Detection and Local-\n\nization\nConsiderable research has been conducted on leakage detection and localiza-\ntion [19]. In general, these approaches are frequently tested in very small bench-\nmark networks and often rely on strongly simplified artificial demands. In this\nwork, we will provide a categorization of approaches (i) that perform leakage de-\ntection and/or localization based on pressure, flow, and demand measurements\nand (ii) that are evaluated in larger networks with realistic demands or real-world\nmeasurements used as we assume better generalization and scalability in these\ncases. We summarize them in Table 1. Due to space constraints, we only include\nthe six best-performing contributions from the BattLeDIM challenge [49].\nAs discussed before, leakages or anomalies more generally can be modeled as\nconcept drift, i.e., as distributional changes in the data generating distribution\n[15]. While some works attempt leakage detection as end-to-end ML, many other\napproaches implement some kind of drift detection scheme.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n(PDF) Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/364293441_Battle_of_the_Leakage_Detection_and_Isolation_Methods)\nciteturn27search5 [wordlim: 200] Published: 4.4 years ago; Crawled: last month; The Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM), was organized in 2020 129 ... In this work, a realistic open benchmark for leakage detection and 126\n\nlocalization is developed and used in a “battle” (Taormina et al. 2018), to allow diﬀerent teams to 127\n\nevaluate their methods in a uniﬁed way.128\n\nThe Battle of Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM), was organized in 2020 129\n\ninitially as part of the CCWI/WDSA 2020 conference (the conference was postponed due to the 130\n\nCOVID-19 pandemic). The competition aimed to objectively compare the performance of methods 131\n\nfor the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on SCADA measurements of ﬂow and 132\n\npressure sensors generated using a realistic virtual city, which was based on a real water distribution 133\n\n5 Vrachimis et al., May 24, 2022\n\nnetwork in Cyprus. The overall objective was to detect as many leakages as possible, as fast as 134\n\npossible and as close to the source as possible, while avoiding false alarms. Huang et al. [11] propose a method consisting of five stages: model decomposition, data partitioning, nodal demand calibration, calibration residual-based leakage detection, and an improved vectorial angle method for leakage localization. The Leakbusters team, represented by Daniel et al. [12], developed a method with two algorithm components. The first component analyzes pressure differences between pairs of nodes using SCADA data to identify leakage events. The model is trained on normal time periods, and the reconstruction error is used to detect leakage events. Saldarriaga et al. [13] utilize a genetic algorithm (GA) to identify leaks, while Wang et al. [14] from the Tsinghua Team employ a multistage approach involving empirical mode decomposition, extraction of daily and weekly seasonalities, and emitter representation. The Under Pressure team, represented by Steffelbauer et al. [15], introduces a hierarchical approach for leak diagnosis. It involves demand calibration using AMR data and measured flows, mathematical optimization for calibrating pipe roughness, and the use of a dual network for leak start time detection and localization. Zhang et al. [16] employ a fuzzy similarity priority ratio for leakage localization, while Romero et al. [17] from the IRI team combine a model-based and data-driven methodology. Their approach determines the usage of the two approaches based on the characteristics of the network’s different areas. Min et al. [18] and Blocher et al. [19] both use clustering algorithms to solve the task, and Liu et al. [20] employ an LSTM. Dopazo et al. [21] and Tan et al. [22] utilize machine learning approaches. The BattLeDIM 2020 dataset [6] was created using a real water distribution network in Cyprus, covering a pipe length of 42.6 km.\n\nWu et al. [24] and Bhowmick et al. [25] both use time series data decomposition, while Marzola et al. [26] spatially localize anomalies through an enumerative procedure. Barros et al. [27] introduce a signal processing approach.\n\nNone of these approaches modeled the task using graph neural networks except for Gardars-son et al. [28], who trained two Chebyshev polynomial kernel Graph Convolutional Networks. Although they stated to gain a better economic score, their results have not been reproducable according to the delivered code and have thus been excluded from the conducted experiments.\n\nOn the other hand, there is an increasing number of papers that use Heterogeneous Graph Neural Networks (HGNN) for link prediction like HetGNN [29] or MTHetGNN [30].\n\n3. Dataset Description\n\nThe BattLeDIM 2020 dataset [6] was created using a real water distribution network in Cyprus, covering a pipe length of 42.6 km. It contains information on pipe breaks, water losses, and two consumer types: residential and commercial, each with distinct demand patterns. The dataset consists of 782 nodes, 33 of them equipped with pressure sensors providing measurements every 5 minutes. Pressure data were obtained from pressure measurements from 33 pressure sensors in the L‐Town network.\nIn this chapter, GCN is integrated with the L‐Town network to conduct modeling experiments on large‐scale networks.\n\n#### Introduction of the L‐Town WDN\n\nL‐Town is a benchmark model widely used in WDN research, initially introduced by BattLeDIM (Dataset of BattLeDIM: Battle of the Leakage Detection & Isolation Methods, 2020). The model provides a realistic yet anonymized WDN, along with extensive historical and evaluation datasets, offering valuable resources for researchers and engineers in the development and assessment of leakage detection and isolation methods.\n\nThe L‐Town dataset includes detailed EPANET model files, comprising nominal parameters, actual network parameters, and historical baseline demands for nodes. In addition to the network model, the dataset provides extensive measurement data from the SCADA system, including: demand, including water consumption measurements from 82 automated meter reading devices; water‐level data, including tank water‐level measurements from 1 water‐level sensor; pressure data, including pressure measurements from 33 pressure sensors.\n\nIn addition, the dataset includes detailed information and time‐series data on leakage events, as well as reports on repaired leaks. These data provide a solid foundation for studying the operation of WDN, developing leakage detection algorithms, and evaluating system performance.\n\n#### Full‐scale WDN model\n\nThis section first conducts anomaly‐state recognition experiments on a full‐scale WDN. Figure 10 illustrates the nodes and topology of the full‐scale network in EPANET. The Figure includes 33 pressure monitoring points (yellow dots), and real demand data are available for all nodes. According to the L‐Town “readme” documentation: The nominal base demands for each node are based on average historical metered consumption. In general, the difference between the actual and the nominal values for each consumer type (residential and commercial) is less than 10%. Pressure data were obtained from pressure measurements from 33 pressure sensors in the L‐Town network. These two types of real data provided by L‐Town were used as the model inputs in this study.\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAn Interpretable Ensemble Learning Framework for Leakage Localization in Urban Water Distribution Networks | MDPI (https://www.mdpi.com/2073-4441/18/17/2220)\nciteturn27search7 [wordlim: 200] Published: last month; Crawled: 3 weeks ago; This study employed the L-Town water distribution model from the BattLeDIM competition [44]. ... In addition, 33 pressure sensors are installed at different nodes within the L-Town network.\n## 3. Results\n\n### 3.1. Data Description\n\nThis study employed the L-Town water distribution model from the BattLeDIM competition [44]. L-Town is a small hypothetical urban WDN consisting of 785 nodes, 909 pipes, one pump, and three valves. In addition, 33 pressure sensors are installed at different nodes within the L-Town network. The L-Town network is adopted as the evaluation case study owing to its established status as an authoritative benchmark from the BattLeDIM competition, which ensures the reproducibility and comparability of our findings against state-of-the-art methodologies. Furthermore, despite its moderate scale, the network features highly heterogeneous topological attributes and integrates critical operational components (such as the pump and valves) capable of replicating complex, realistic hydraulic dynamics. Crucially, the deployment of 33 pressure sensors against 785 nodes represents a highly sparse sensing configuration. This alignment with the practical budgetary and infrastructural constraints of real-world water utilities provides a rigorous environment to validate the robustness of the proposed leakage localization framework.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n(PDF) A Multivariate Statistical Framework for Detection, Classification and Pre-localization of Anomalies in Water Distribution Networks (https://www.researchgate.net/publication/400578051_A_Multivariate_Statistical_Framework_for_Detection_Classification_and_Pre-localization_of_Anomalies_in_Water_Distribution_Networks)\nciteturn27search8 [wordlim: 200] Published: 8 months ago; Crawled: last month; L-Town benchmark from the BattLeDIM 2020 competition (Vrachimis ... Abstract  | This paper presents a unified framework for the detection, classification, and preliminary localization of anomalies in water distribution networks using multivariate statistical analysis.\n\n# A Multivariate Statistical Framework for Detection, Classification and Pre-localization of Anomalies in Water Distribution Networks\n\nImage: Open Access\n\nAuthors  | \n\n  * Oleg Melnikov\n  * Yurii Dorofieiev\n  * Yurii Shakhnovskiy\n  * Huy Truong\nShow more\n\n  * Victoria Degeler\n  * Show less\nPublication date  | 01-06-2026\nJournal  | Expert Systems With Applications\nArticle number  | 131450\nVolume | Issue number  | 313\nNumber of pages  | 21\nOrganisations  | \n\n  * Faculty of Science (FNWI) - Informatics Institute (IVI)\nAbstract  | This paper presents a unified framework for the detection, classification, and preliminary localization of anomalies in water distribution networks using multivariate statistical analysis. The approach, termed SICAMS (Statistical Identification and Classification of Anomalies in Mahalanobis Space), processes heterogeneous pressure and flow sensor data through a whitening transformation to eliminate spatial correlations among measurements. Based on the transformed data, the Hotelling’s T2 statistic is constructed, enabling the formulation of anomaly detection as a statistical hypothesis test of network conformity to normal operating conditions. It is shown that Hotelling’s T2 statistic can serve as an integral indicator of the overall “health” of the system, exhibiting correlation with total leakage volume, and thereby enabling approximate estimation of water losses via a regression model. A heuristic algorithm is developed to analyze the T2 time series and classify detected anomalies into abrupt leaks, incipient leaks, and sensor malfunctions. Furthermore, a coarse leak localization method is proposed, which ranks sensors according to their statistical contribution and employs Laplacian interpolation to approximate the affected region within the network. Application of the proposed framework to the BattLeDIM L-Town benchmark dataset demonstrates high sensitivity and reliability in leak detection, maintaining robust performance even under multiple leaks. These capabilities make the method applicable to real-world operational environments without the need for a calibrated hydraulic model. Water demand fore-\r\ncasting has been also addressed using GNNs (Zanfei, Brentan, et al., 2022). Metamodeling is another interesting\r\narea where GNNs have been leveraged for the estimation of pressures and flows (Kerimov et al., 2023; Lima\r\net al., 2018; Zanfei et al., 2023). GNN‐based models has been also used to solve problems related to water quality.\r\nFor example, Z. Li et al. (2024a) propose GNNs for identifying contamination sources in water distribution\r\nsystems, and for water quality prediction (Z. Li et al., 2024b). Other recent works on GNNs for pressure esti-\r\nmation are (Ashraf et al., 2023; Hajgató et al., 2021a, 2021b). The main differences of those approaches with ours\r\nare the training strategy and the evaluation protocol to assess the model performance. Our proposed techniques in\r\nthis regard are more realistic and give the model the ability to adapt to unexpected changes.\r\nIn this work, we focus on pressure estimation by leveraging both physics‐based simulation models and GNN‐\r\nbased data‐driven approaches. We rely on a data generation method that leverages the EPANET simulation\r\ntool to overcome the lack of data required for model training. However, in our approach we include all dynamic\r\nparameters (e.g., reservoir total heads, tank levels, roughness coefficient) which were not considered in previous\r\nworks. This contributes to data variety and avoids that uncertainties propagate due to model simplification errors\r\n(Du et al., 2018). Our main contributions are twofold. First, our GNN‐based estimation model is robust to un-\r\nexpected sensor's location changes due to the proposed training strategy that relies on random sensor placement.\r\nSecond, our evaluation protocol considers real time‐dependent patterns and additionally injects the uncertainties\r\nintrinsic to real‐world scenarios. The outcome is a new state‐of‐the‐art GNN‐based model, GAT with Residual\r\nConnections (GATRes), for pressure estimation in WDNs.\r\nGATRes is able to reconstruct the junction pressures of Oosterbeek, a large‐scale WDN in the Netherlands, with\r\nan average 1.94 m absolute error, which represents an 8.57% improvement with respect to other models. Simi-\r\nlarly, our model outperformed previous approaches on other WDNs benchmark data sets. The highest\r\nimprovement was seen for C‐Town WDN (Ostfeld et al., 2012) with an absolute error decrease of 52.36%, for\r\nRichmond (Van Zyl, 2001) an error decrease of 5.31%, and 40.35% error decrease for L‐Town (Vrachimis\r\net al., 2022). In addition, our first attempt on model generalization shows that a multi‐graph pre‐training followed\r\nby fine‐tuning helps to increase the model performance. The absolute error on Oosterbeek network was reduced\r\nby ≈2% following our generalization strategy.\r\nThe remainder of this document is as follows. Section 2 presents the problem statement, describes the issues that\r\nneed to be addressed by pressure reconstruction models and defines the criteria to assess the model capabilities.\r\nSection 3 depicts the related work in the field, narrowed to GNNs for node‐level regression tasks and how\r\nprevious work on GNN‐based pressure estimation satisfies the criteria defined in the Section 2. The methodology\r\nis presented in Section 4, including the data generation process, a detailed description of our model architecture,\r\nand the details of the proposed approach for model training and evaluation. Section 5 describes the setup of the\r\nexperimental phase. It includes a description of WDNs benchmark data sets used in this work, the base model\r\nconfigurations, and the evaluation metrics. Section 6 describes all the empirical evaluations of our approach. First,\r\nthe experiments on the main use case of this study, Oosterbeek WDN, are depicted. Then, the experiments toward\r\nmodel generalization are shown. Next, the performance of the proposed model on different benchmark WDNs is\r\npresented. This section concludes with an ablation study to identify the contribution of the different components\r\nof the model architecture. A discussion of the most salient findings are presented in Section 7. Finally, the\r\nconclusions are presented in Section 8.\r\n2. Pressure Estimation in Water Distribution Networks\r\n2.1. Problem Statement\r\nHydraulic experts have managed WDNs using essential measurements such as flow, demand, and pressure. These\r\nmeasurements offer a comprehensive perspective of a WDN, forming a foundation for various supervisory tasks\r\nlike forecasting (Iwakin & Moazeni, 2024), leak detection (Garðarsson et al., 2022), and operational control\r\nWater Resources Research\r\n10.1029/2023WR036741\r\nTRUONG ET AL.\r\n2 of 27\r\n19447973, 2024, 7, Downloaded from https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1029/2023WR036741 by Uva Universiteitsbibliotheek, Wiley Online Library on [10/07/2024]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License\r\nPAGE 3\r\n(Nerantzis et al., 2020). For this reason, this study focuses on addressing the fundamental challenge of\r\napproximating measurements across all nodal locations within water networks. In this investigation, we initially\r\nassume that the prior knowledge (i.e., historical data) is unavailable and network states are discretely recorded\r\nunder typical conditions, disregarding unforeseen events like leaks, earthquakes, or fires. Additionally, we\r\npresume that measured states between two neighborhoods within the network exhibit a degree of similarity. These\r\nassumptions are crucial for employing a data‐driven machine learning model trained from valid cases while\r\navoiding corruption in the complexities of water networks.\r\nA real‐life WDN consists of diverse components such as tanks, valves, pumps, reservoirs, and thousands of\r\ncustomer junctions whose measurement states are crucial for management. In this study, we narrow the scope and\r\nfavor pressure as the primary measurement due to the ease of meter installation and the more affordable price\r\ncompared to flow ones (Zhou et al., 2019). Nevertheless, these pressure sensors are limited in practice due to\r\ninfrastructural limits and privacy concerns. Consequently, the gathered data, known as the pressure states of the\r\njunction nodes, are scarce. These states play crucial roles as samples in training data‐driven approaches aligned\r\nwith a machine‐learning model that often requires a large amount of data. As a prerequisite, pressure states should\r\nbe fully observable to minimize the estimation loss of the trained model in all customer positions throughout the\r\nwater network. This contradiction poses a challenge in applying the machine‐learning approach to solve pressure\r\nestimation tasks.\r\nThe application context is about what and when the trained model should be applied. Generally, a model is often\r\nassociated with a unique water network and fixed sensors previously seen during training. Also, the training\r\nenvironment may exclude noisy, uncertain conditions that could affect the model's decision‐making. In other\r\nwords, these challenges result in worse model performance when faced with unfamiliar network topologies or\r\nuncertain situations. Consequently, model retraining is inevitable, albeit such training is an expensive and un-\r\nsustainable approach. This concern enhances the necessity of the generalization ability of pressure estimation\r\nmodels, which needs to be addressed in prior research. Before addressing this research gap, we will first delve into\r\nthe specific problem within water networks and lay out the criteria necessary for a robust pressure estimation\r\nmodel.\r\n2.2. Partially‐Observable Data and Realistic Model Evaluation\r\nWater distribution networks domain is characterized by partial‐observability due to the limited sensor coverage.\r\nThis imposes an additional challenge because the reconstruction models need to be trained on fully‐observable\r\nnetwork operation snapshots. The common approach to overcome this limitation is to rely on mathematical\r\nhydraulic simulation tools, for example, EPANET (Rossman, 1999), to generate full‐views of the network\r\noperation and use them for model training (Ashraf et al., 2023; Hajgató et al., 2021a, 2021b; Xing & Sela, 2022;\r\nZhou et al., 2023).\r\nAlthough the hydraulic simulations solve the lack of training data for the reconstruction models, the remaining\r\nchallenge is how to create a valid and reliable evaluation protocol and the data used for it. Sampling from the data\r\ngenerated from the simulation models and splitting them into training and test sets is not enough. Ideally, the\r\nassumption behind machine learning models is that the training data is ruled by the exact same distribution of the\r\ndata on which the model will be evaluated. However, having absolute control over the data generation process and\r\nmeeting such a perfect match between both distributions is unrealistic and the assumption is violated under real‐\r\nworld conditions (Bickel et al., 2007; Fang et al., 2022; Hendrycks & Gimpel, 2017). Thus, the prediction models\r\nshould be robust to distribution shifts between training and testing samples, that is, be able to generalize to out‐of‐\r\ndistribution (OOD) data (Farquhar & Gal, 2022).\r\nWe observed that the data distribution of pressures in the training and test sets created by the simulation models\r\nare identical, which is unnatural in practice. The density distributions of pressures in the training and test sets from\r\ndifferent WDNs, generated by the Hydraulic Simulation tool EPANET, are shown in Figure 1. In this case, the\r\nsimulation's dynamic parameters, for example, reservoir total heads, junction demands, pump speed, were\r\nrandomly adjusted for every run. As we described before, we consider only WDN states under normal conditions.\r\nThus, all static parameters, for example, node elevation, pipe length, pipe diameter, etc. are fixed. Each simulation\r\nrun produces an arbitrary state of the network (snapshot) given a particular combination of input parameter's\r\nvalues. The algorithm ran until 50,000 stable network states were reached. Then, the data were randomly par-\r\ntitioned into training, validation, and test set in a 60:20:20 ratio, respectively. The plots were generated taking\r\nWater Resources Research\r\n10.1029/2023WR036741\r\nTRUONG ET AL.\r\n3 of 27\r\n19447973, 2024, 7, Downloaded from https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1029/2023WR036741 by Uva Universiteitsbibliotheek, Wiley Online Library on [10/07/2024]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License\r\nPAGE 4\r\n2,000 snapshots at random from the training and test sets, where each snapshot represent the pressures at all\r\njunctions. Nonetheless, as evident from the image, the distributions of the training and test sets created by the\r\nhydraulic simulations are identical in all examples. In this case, evaluating the reconstruction models on data\r\ngenerated by the same algorithm is simply evaluating the ability of the model to reconstruct the signals already\r\nseen during the training process.\r\nIn our work we propose a realistic test set generation process that relies on time‐based demand patterns. In\r\naddition, Gaussian noise is injected before the simulation to mimic the uncertainty intrinsic to real‐world sce-\r\nnarios. Combining time‐based demand patterns and noise injection allows to create realistic scenarios to evaluate\r\nthe ability of the models to generalize to OOD data, with visible differences in density distribution between\r\ntraining and test sets.\r\n2.3. Criteria for Model Assessment\r\nThe out‐of‐distribution problem may originate from the uncertainty of measured sensors, the fluctuation in hy-\r\ndraulic parameters, and the diversity of water network topologies. Within the context of these factors, we delve\r\ninto the limitations of physical models. First, the uncertainty from sensors is due to the gradual sensor coverage\r\nplan, maintenance period, and sensor malfunction. This uncertainty impacts the performance of physical models\r\nand often requires a human‐involved recalibration process. Similarly, unforeseen fluctuations in hydraulic pa-\r\nrameters can be triggered by changes in socioeconomic conditions, aging infrastructure, or unexpected events like\r\npandemics. While existing techniques on top of the physical model, such as calibration and skeletonization, can\r\nmitigate these issues, they often require further human intervention. Additionally, encountering unseen water\r\nnetworks with different topologies inevitably leads to a redesign process that costs time and resources signifi-\r\ncantly. Furthermore, the physical model can only perform a proper simulation if all hydraulic parameters of all\r\ncomponents in the new network are fully identified, posing a practical challenge and emphasizing the persistence\r\nof a generalization problem.\r\nSome previous studies have proposed utilizing more efficient data‐driven machine‐learning models (discussed in\r\nSection 3) to tackle the challenges above. However, it is critical to note that these works have often overlooked\r\nsome of these problems. This oversight essentially motivates us to have a list of criteria that indicate the favorable\r\ncapabilities of a data‐driven model addressing the pressure estimation task on WDNs. We then propose the criteria\r\nof generalizability, adaptability, and robustness as follows.\r\n(C1) Generalizability: The model is able to perform the pressure estimation task across diverse WDNs,\r\nregardless of their topology, even when encountering networks not seen during training. This is an important\r\naspect of generalizability, making it more useful in practice.\r\n(C2) Adaptability: The model should efficiently maintain its estimation capabilities in diverse contextual\r\ncircumstances, typically involving the positional variation of sensor measurements. Sensor‐related events\r\nlike periodic maintenance, gradual coverage plans, and malfunctions can affect changes in sensor locations\r\nwithin the network, leading to exhaustive retraining for conventional machine‐learning models in severe\r\nFigure 1. Density distribution of training and test sets in C‐Town, Richmond, L‐Town, and Oosterbeek water distribution networks, generated by the hydraulic\r\nsimulation tool EPANET.\r\nWater Resources Research\r\n10.1029/2023WR036741\r\nTRUONG ET AL.\r\n4 of 27\r\n19447973, 2024, 7, Downloaded from https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1029/2023WR036741 by Uva Universiteitsbibliotheek, Wiley Online Library on [10/07/2024]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License\r\nPAGE 5\r\ncases. Therefore, the criterion favoring model flexibility is essential to uphold the model performance during\r\nthe network's life cycle.\r\n(C3) Robustness: This criterion considers the model's robustness against inherent distortion in the wild. The\r\nreasons involve noise in observations, data transmission, and parameter discrepancy between simulated and\r\nactual environments. Notably, this uncertainty has been overlooked in existing approaches, as testing data\r\nwas often evaluated in a noise‐free, laboratory environment.\r\nIn the following section, the provided list is crucial in assessing the state‐of‐the‐art methods for the pressure\r\nestimation task. Accordingly, we outline their accomplishments and limitations before presenting our solution\r\nthat purposely fulfills all specified criteria.\r\n3. Related Work\r\n3.1. GNNs for Node‐Level Regression Task\r\nMost of the GNNs have originated from a feed‐forward neural network involving multiple hidden layers of\r\ninterconnected neurons. Each layer performs a transformation of the input data and then passes it through a series\r\nof mathematical operations. In terms of GNNs, the favored input data is the graph. As it can naturally represent an\r\nabstract level of a WDN, we purposely investigated existing GNN approaches for solving our core problem\r\nknown as node‐level regression.\r\nWu et al. (2021) categorized GNN based on their purposes into graph‐level, link‐level, and node‐level tasks.\r\nThese categories indicate the versatility and primary focus of GNN to provide outcomes across various domains.\r\nFor instance, graph‐level and link‐level have been employed in domains such as chemistry (Reiser et al., 2022),\r\nbioinformatics (Nguyen et al., 2020), and recommendation systems (Z. Chen et al., 2020). On the other hand, the\r\nnode classification task has risen the prominence in the node‐level category, demonstrating its dominance in\r\npractical fields such as physics (Shlomi et al., 2020) and finance (B. Xu et al., 2021).\r\nAs an influence of prevalent node classification, well‐known GNN architectures have been developed to excel for\r\nthis specific task (M. Chen et al., 2020; Defferrard et al., 2016; Veličković et al., 2018). This leads to the lack of\r\nattention to node regression tasks and causes ambiguity regarding the effectiveness of these popular GNNs in\r\nhandling continuous values within the node‐level regime. While early research has explored node‐level regression\r\nin narrow domains (Derrow‐Pinion et al., 2021; Ying et al., 2018), comprehensive comparisons across these\r\npopular architectures, especially within the water sector, are lacking. Addressing this issue, we delve into\r\nexploring the capability of popular GNNs in tackling a fundamental challenge in node regression: state\r\nestimation.\r\n3.2. State Estimation With GNNs\r\nEarly research integrated GNN (GNNs) into calibration processes (Zanfei et al., 2023). Along with the devel-\r\nopment, recent advancements have introduced calibration‐free GNNs, showcasing notably superior performance\r\ncompared to classical models in state estimation tasks (Hajgató et al., 2021a, 2021b). State estimation is a process\r\nof inferring the current state of a WDN. For example, it involves predicting the water pressure or flow rate at\r\ndifferent nodes based on sensor measurements within the network. Its application is crucial for subsequent\r\nmanagement tasks, including leak localization (Mücke et al., 2023), optimal control (Martínez et al., 2007), and\r\ncyber‐attack detection (Taormina et al., 2018).\r\nIt is worth distinguishing state estimation from a related concept known as surrogate modeling, as both ap-\r\nproaches may produce similar outputs, such as pressure, flow rate, and velocity (Kerimov et al., 2023). The key\r\ndistinction lies in their objectives and input features. Surrogate models strive to replicate the behavior of a\r\nphysical simulation model across diverse input parameters—such as customer demand, nodal elevation, and\r\npump head patterns. Conversely, state estimation focuses on reconstructing signals at unmeasured nodes based on\r\nexisting ones and the network's topology. In other words, state estimation models have fewer requirements to\r\nprovide outcomes, leading to beneficial convenience in the practical application. Expanding from this point, we\r\nsupply a comprehensive list of representative works and assess them against the predefined criteria outlined in\r\nSection 2.3.\r\nWater Resources Research\r\n10.1029/2023WR036741\r\nTRUONG ET AL.\r\n5 of 27\r\n19447973, 2024, 7, Downloaded from https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1029/2023WR036741 by Uva Universiteitsbibliotheek, Wiley Online Library on [10/07/2024]. See the Terms and Conditions (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/terms-and-conditions) on Wiley Online Library for rules of use; OA articles are governed by the applicable Creative Commons License\r\n"

Index Terms—leak localization, water distribution network, state estimation, interpolation I. INTRODUCTION W ATER resources are becoming increasingly scarce while global demand continues to rise, with projec-\r\np.1: been detected through night flow analysis and localized using portable acoustic sensors. However, this is inefficient for large- scale systems, as inspection is limited to reduced sections of the WDN at a time. This limitation has driven the demand for software-based solutions that can automatically analyze sensor data using models and algorithms, enabling simultaneous real- time, system-wide monitoring. Most leak localization methods are model-based, relying on hydraulic models to simulate the behaviour of the WDN. Simulated hydraulic data is then compared to actual mea- surements to localize the leak. Common approaches include analyzing pressure sensitivity to leaks [3] or solving the inverse hydraulic problem, applying optimization techniques\r\np.1: neering, Politehnica University of Bucharest, e-mail: florin.stoican@upb.ro. to calibrate network parameters or nodal consumption from pressure and flow data [4]. Model-based methods can achieve high accuracy if the hydraulic model is well-calibrated, but this is a challenging task given the complexity of networks, mathematical models and potential modeling errors. Advancements in data analysis and machine learning helped to reduce the reliance on well-calibrated hydraulic models while achieving comparable node-level accuracy1 to model- based methods, leading to the development of mixed model- based/data-driven methods. They typically use the model to generate training samples for the potential leaks at different network conditions, to then learn from these data. Common\r\np.1: or demand sensors, normally denoted as Automated Meter Reading (AMR) [14]. Therefore, a natural step to improve state interpolation involves integrating all the available mea- surements in the WDN. To address the limitations of GSI and AW-GSI, which cannot handle the non-linear relation between heads and flow/demand, we recently proposed an upgrade of these methods that fuses pressure and demand information through an Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) [15], referred to as UKF-(AW)GSI [16]. 1In the context of leak localization, accuracy can be classified as “node- level”, which implies identifying the exact network element with the leak, or “area-level”, where a broader search area is indicated. arXiv:2412.11687v1 [eess.SY] 16 Dec 2024\r\np.2: one estimator is used to feed the measurement vector of the other. This allows both processes to yield a consistent dual solution for heads and flows. The performance of our method is tested over the Modena [17] and L-TOWN benchmarks [18]. Hydraulic state estimation Within the context of leak localization, the estimation of the hydraulic state of a WDN consists in using hydraulic informa- tion available from existing network-wide sensors to retrieve\r\np.3: considering only first-order terms in the Jacobian linearization. b) Unscented Kalman Filter: Recently, the Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) [15] emerged as an alternative to EKF, mitigating its limitations without adding extra computational cost. UKF utilizes the actual non-linear models, approximating the distribution of the random variable representing the state. Unlike EKF, which propagates only the state mean and covari- ance, UKF propagates a set of ”sigma” points from the original distribution through the non-linear model. Each UKF iteration is composed of three stages, namely prediction, measurement propagation and correction. The prediction\r\np.4: Sensor fusion methods, such as the KF strategies described in Section II-B, are a solution for integrating measurements in WDNs. They can be coupled with interpolation strategies like GSI or AW-GSI to improve estimation accuracy: the state vector reconstructed by any of these interpolation methods is used as the initial guess for the KF algorithm, which iterates between the state prediction and data assimilation steps until the stop condition is met. A. Improving state estimation through KF An initial integration of GSI/AW-GSI with Kalman Filter based methods can be attained by considering the linear case. The method employs pressure measurements and is based on four pillars: (1)\r\n4\r\nhydraulic model-based generation of leak-free training data by taking into account the demand\r\n5\r\nuncertainty, (2) conversion of hydraulic model input demand-output pressure pairs into images\r\n6\r\nusing kriging interpolation, (3) training of a CDCGAN model for image-to-image translation, and\r\n7\r\n(4) using the structural similarity (SSIM) index for LD&L. SSIM, computed over the entire\r\n8\r\npressure distribution image is used for leak detection, and a local estimate of SSIM is employed\r\n9\r\nfor leak localization. The CDCGAN model employed in this paper is based on the pix2pix\r\n10\r\narchitecture. The effectiveness of the proposed methodology is demonstrated on leakage datasets\r\n11\r\nunder various scenarios. Results show that the method has an accuracy of approximately 70% for\r\n12\r\nreal-time leak detection. The proposed method is well-suited for real-time applications due to the\r\n13\r\nlow computational cost of CDCGAN predictions compared to WDN hydraulic models, is robust\r\n14\r\nin presence of uncertainty due to the nature of generative adversarial networks, and scales well to\r\n15\r\nlarge and variable-sized monitoring data due to the use of an image-based approach.\r\n16\r\nKeywords: Leak; anomaly detection; generative adversarial networks; image-to-image translation;\r\n17\r\nstructural similarity index.\r\n18\r\n1. Introduction\r\n19\r\nWith the rapid development of supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) technologies,\r\n20\r\nreal-time monitoring of hydraulic parameters is becoming increasingly commonplace in many\r\n21\r\nwater distribution networks (WDNs) (Zhou et al., 2019). Monitoring is often performed by\r\n22\r\ninstalling pressure and flow sensors in the transmission mains, allowing for an improved\r\n23\r\nunderstanding of system behavior, and diagnosis of anomalous events, the most notable of which\r\n24\r\nis pipe leaks. In this context, diagnosis of leakage often includes at least two aspects: (a) ‘leakage\r\n25\r\ndetection’, which is to detect if leakage has occurred, and usually ends with binary results (i.e. leak\r\n26\r\nalarm on or off), or fuzzy values and probabilities between 0 to 1 to represent the likelihood of a\r\n27\r\nleakage event in the system (Mounce et al., 2010). (b) ‘Leak localization’ (or isolation) is the\r\n28\r\nprocess of narrowing down the potential location of a leak to a specific area or district. The latter\r\n29\r\nPAGE 3\r\n3\r\n\r\nis a precursor to finding the leaky pipe(s) and then pinpointing the exact leakage location (Al\r\n30\r\nQahtani et al. 2020).\r\n31\r\n1.1. Literature Review\r\n32\r\nSeveral past studies (e.g. Kang et al., 2017; Guo et al., 2021) rely on auxiliary data such as acoustic\r\n33\r\nand vibration signals, for more accurate LD&L in presence of nuisance factors. Despite the\r\n34\r\neffectiveness, obtaining such data is labor intensive, and the quality of these data can be affected\r\n35\r\nby background noise and other events in the system or its environment (Wu and Liu, 2017).\r\n36\r\nLeakages in pipeline systems can also be detected using transient analysis by relying on the\r\n37\r\nprinciple of pressure transient wave reflection. A comprehensive overview of these methods was\r\n38\r\nprovided by Abdulshaheed et al. (2017). However, the effectiveness of transient analysis-based\r\n39\r\ndetection techniques is heavily influenced by pipe characteristics and external factors (Kammoun\r\n40\r\net al., 2022). Furthermore, this approach is unsuitable for detecting leaks over long pipe distances\r\n41\r\nsince pressure waves only propagate short distances. Additionally, implementing this technique\r\n42\r\noften requires intricate mathematical algorithms and substantial computational resources (Wan et\r\n43\r\nal., 2022).\r\n44\r\nPressure and flow sensors have the advantage of being easy to install, enabling real-time\r\n45\r\nmonitoring, and being sensitive to leakage events. Therefore, leakage detection and localization\r\n46\r\n(LD&L) using pressure/flow data has received increasing attention in the past decades. LD&L\r\n47\r\nusing pressure/flow data can be done through one of the following two groups of methods. The\r\n48\r\ndistinguishing factor between the two is whether a hydraulic model is used or not (Wan et al.,\r\n49\r\n2022).\r\n50\r\nA. ‘Data-driven’ methods rely on historical monitoring data and involve spatial analysis of\r\n51\r\nchanges across a WDN, or temporal pattern analysis of time series data. Methods in this\r\n52\r\ngroup may involve: (1) defining control limits by calculating some statistical characteristic\r\n53\r\n(e.g. exponentially weighted moving average) of historical measurements and defining data\r\n54\r\nthat is outside these limits as potential leaks (e.g. Jung et al., 2015; Loureiro et al., 2016).\r\n55\r\nThese methods often rely on assumptions such as the Gaussianity of data distribution and\r\n56\r\nthe uncorrelation of errors. (2) Using historical data to train a predictor model that learns\r\n57\r\nthe expected behavior of the system in leak-free circumstances, and comparing the\r\n58\r\nresiduals between the observed and predicted value with a threshold to identify leaks. A\r\n59\r\nvariety of machine learning tools, such as support vector machines and classic neural\r\n60\r\nPAGE 4\r\n4\r\n\r\nnetworks have been employed in this context (e.g. Mounce et al., 2011; Romano et al.,\r\n61\r\n2012; Tijani et al., 2022; Tariq et al., 2022). (3) Reframing the spatial/temporal pattern\r\n62\r\nrecognition as a classification problem (using e.g. Bayesian classifiers) (e.g. Wu et al.,\r\n63\r\n2016). This method requires labeled data, i.e. an indication of whether a chosen data\r\n64\r\ninstance pertains to a leak or not. (4) Formulating LD&L as a clustering problem by\r\n65\r\ngrouping similar data into different clusters, and identifying leaks as those that are\r\n66\r\ndissimilar to normal cluster(s) (e.g. Wu and Liu, 2020).\r\n67\r\nB. ‘Model-based’ methods, also known as physically-based or process-based methods, rely\r\n68\r\non the comparison of measured data with those of a calibrated WDN hydraulic model.\r\n69\r\nModel-based methods include: (1) formulating LD&L as an inverse modeling problem\r\n70\r\nusing an optimization algorithm, and one or multiple objective function(s) (e.g. Sanz et al.,\r\n71\r\n2016). (2) Relying on sensitivity-to-leak analysis (e.g. Perez et al., 2014), where model-\r\n72\r\ngenerated pressure disturbances caused by all possible leak locations and magnitudes are\r\n73\r\nstored in a leak-sensitivity matrix and matched against the difference between measured\r\n74\r\nand simulated data (Soldevila et al., 2017). (3) Generating pressure or pressure residual\r\n75\r\nmaps of each leakage scenario using the hydraulic model, employing the resulting data to\r\n76\r\ntrain a classifier, and finally utilizing the trained classifier for LD&L. The latter is\r\n77\r\nsometimes referred to as a mixed model-based/data-driven approach (Soldevila et al.,\r\n78\r\n2016).\r\n79\r\nBoth data-driven and model-based methods have their advantages and limitations, and it is\r\n80\r\nunfeasible to draw a conclusion about which method is universally superior (Wan et al., 2022).\r\n81\r\nData-driven approaches directly incorporate experimental data, and hence don’t require a deep\r\n82\r\nknowledge of the system operation and physical equations. Data-driven methods are best suited\r\n83\r\nfor WDNs with a long-term monitoring dataset and were found to be effective in leak detection.\r\n84\r\nHowever, their performance in leak localization is debated (Wu and Liu, 2017). It is generally\r\n85\r\ndifficult to obtain enough correct labeled data to train a high-accuracy model in a supervised\r\n86\r\nmanner (Zhang et al., 2020); therefore data-driven LD&L methods often rely on unsupervised or\r\n87\r\nsemi-supervised training. Many commonly used tools within the context of the data-driven\r\n88\r\napproach have limitations in learning complex features and using them requires the manual design\r\n89\r\nof suitable feature extractors (Zhou et al., 2019). Data-driven methods are particularly vulnerable\r\n90\r\nto missing or faulty data (Hu et al., 2021).\r\n91\r\nPAGE 5\r\n5\r\n\r\nModel-based methods are preferred when limited historical data is available, but come at the cost\r\n92\r\nof building and calibrating a hydraulic model. Moreover, the high computation demand of\r\n93\r\nhydraulic models often hinders achieving real-time LD&L. A common challenge for both model-\r\n94\r\nbased and data-driven methods is that pressure and flow rate can be affected by other factors apart\r\n95\r\nfrom leakages, such as variability and stochasticity in demand and random errors in sensor\r\n96\r\nmeasurements. These are often difficult to distinguish from leaks. For model-based methods, the\r\n97\r\neffect of input parameter uncertainty on the model output flow and pressure, adds to this\r\n98\r\ncomplexity (Menapace et al., 2018; Sun et al., 2019). Hence model-based methods do not perform\r\n99\r\nwell in larger WDNs where the models' output uncertainty is often higher compared to smaller\r\n100\r\nWDNs (Zhou et al., 2019). The performance of the model-based sensitivity-to-leak analysis\r\n101\r\nmethod is particularly well-known to decrease due to the nodal demand uncertainty and noise in\r\n102\r\nthe measurements (Cugueró-Escofet et al., 2015).\r\n103\r\nThe above-described challenges have forced previous LD&L studies to focus mainly on\r\n104\r\nhypothetical burst events in simple WDNs (Wan et al., 2022). As highlighted by recent review\r\n105\r\npapers (e.g. Gupta and Kulat, 2018; Chan et al., 2018; Wan et al., 2022), more effort is needed to\r\n106\r\nsolve these challenges by developing novel methods that are: (a) robust in presence of various\r\n107\r\nforms of uncertainty, (b) can scale to very large monitoring datasets, (c) can learn complex features\r\n108\r\nfrom raw data, (d) rely less on manual design, and (e) are well-adapted to a limitation in labeled\r\n109\r\ntraining data.\r\n110\r\nA promising approach to achieve this objective is to exploit deep neural networks (DNNs). DNNs\r\n111\r\nrely on hierarchical projections of the input space into increasingly low-dimensional latent\r\n112\r\nrepresentations (Goodfellow et al., 2016), enabling them to learn complex features from large\r\n113\r\namounts of data without the need for developing manual features by domain experts (Chalapathy\r\n114\r\nand Chawla, 2019). In recent years, several DNN-based anomaly detection methods have been\r\n115\r\nintroduced for a variety of applications, demonstrating significantly better performance than\r\n116\r\nconventional anomaly detection with an increase in the scale of data and complexity of the problem\r\n117\r\n(Pang et al., 2021). Example applications of DNNs in LD&L include the use of convolutional\r\n118\r\nneural networks (CNNs) for burst localization based on data from short-duration pressure\r\n119\r\nobservations (Zhou et al., 2019); supervised training of a CNN using hydraulic model-generated\r\n120\r\nsamples of all possible leaks for leak localization (Javadiha et al., 2019). These approaches, like\r\n121\r\nmany older methods, require a well-calibrated hydraulic model to generate synthetic data that\r\n122\r\nPAGE 6\r\n6\r\n\r\naccurately represent real-life situations for training a deep learning model. However, acquiring and\r\n123\r\nmaintaining a well-calibrated hydraulic model can be challenging for water companies.\r\n124\r\nRegression analysis has been used as an alternative method to learn the data patterns representing\r\n125\r\nthe healthy state of a WDN from historical monitoring data instead of a hydraulic model. As an\r\n126\r\nexample, Ye and Fenner (2011) utilized the Kalman filter to fit historical flow measurements and\r\n127\r\nidentify burst events by comparing predicted and observed values, while also employing the\r\n128\r\nweighted least squares regression to model the data. However, these approaches treat each data\r\n129\r\npoint of a day independently, and thus, they do not fully consider the autocorrelation of the time\r\n130\r\nseries data.\r\n131\r\nMounce et al. (2010) employed a neural network to learn the normal flow behavior from\r\n132\r\nmonitoring data and detect leaks based on prediction errors. A key limitation of their approach is\r\n133\r\nthe inability to share features across different steps of a time series, whereas temporal pattern\r\n134\r\nrecognition requires the ability to process evolving patterns. To address this problem, recurrent\r\n135\r\nneural networks such as long short-term memory (LSTM) networks have been explored for\r\n136\r\nleakage detection (Wang et al., 2020; Xu et al., 2020). While the results of these LSTM-based\r\n137\r\nmethods have shown high detection accuracy, there are still some limitations that need to be\r\n138\r\naddressed. Specifically, these methods are often restricted to univariate time series analysis, which\r\n139\r\nonly allows for the analysis of data from a single sensor at a time. However, it's known that data\r\n140\r\nfrom sensors in the same network are spatially correlated, and considering topology information\r\n141\r\nand the spatial relation of the sensors is essential for accurate leak detection.\r\n142\r\n1.2. In this work, we will be leveraging some of the most recent advances in DNNs, to develop\r\n146\r\na robust methodology for achieving accurate real-time LD&L in complex settings. Our\r\n147\r\nmethodology is based on the use of ‘conditional convolutional generative adversarial networks’\r\n148\r\n(CDCGAN) for image-based anomaly detection. Partially similar methods have been used in other\r\n149\r\napplications for anomaly detection (e.g. Ezeme et al., 2020; Luo et al., 2021; Qiu et al., 2022), but\r\n150\r\nto the best of our knowledge, this method has not been applied to leak detection in WDNs.\r\n151\r\nRelying on flow measurements for LD&L is often easier than pressure measurements, because\r\n152\r\nflow data allows for the use of simple mass balance relations, and pressure data is less sensitive to\r\n153\r\n"

The latter is sometimes referred to as a mixed model-based/data-driven approach (Soldevila et al., 2016). Both data-driven and model-based methods have their advantages and limitations, and it is unfeasible to draw a conclusion about which method is universally superior (Wan et al., 2022). Data-driven approaches directly incorporate experimental data, and hence don’t require a deep knowledge of the system operation and physical equations. Data-driven methods are best suited for WDNs with a long-term monitoring dataset and were found to be effective in leak detection. However, their performance in leak localization is debated (Wu and Liu, 2017). It is generally difficult to obtain enough correct labeled data to train a high-accuracy model in a supervised manner (Zhang et al., 2020); therefore data-driven LD&L methods often rely on unsupervised or semi-supervised training. A promising approach to achieve this objective is to exploit deep neural networks (DNNs). DNNs rely on hierarchical projections of the input space into increasingly low-dimensional latent representations (Goodfellow et al., 2016), enabling them to learn complex features from large amounts of data without the need for developing manual features by domain experts (Chalapathy and Chawla, 2019). In recent years, several DNN-based anomaly detection methods have been introduced for a variety of applications, demonstrating significantly better performance than conventional anomaly detection with an increase in the scale of data and complexity of the problem (Pang et al., 2021). Example applications of DNNs in LD&L include the use of convolutional\r\np.6: accurately represent real-life situations for training a deep learning model. However, acquiring and maintaining a well-calibrated hydraulic model can be challenging for water companies. However, these approaches treat each data point of a day independently, and thus, they do not fully consider the autocorrelation of the time series data. Mounce et al. (2010) employed a neural network to learn the normal flow behavior from\r\np.6: recognition requires the ability to process evolving patterns. To address this problem, recurrent neural networks such as long short-term memory (LSTM) networks have been explored for leakage detection (Wang et al., 2020; Xu et al., 2020). While the results of these LSTM-based methods have shown high detection accuracy, there are still some limitations that need to be addressed. Specifically, these methods are often restricted to univariate time series analysis, which only allows for the analysis of data from a single sensor at a time. However, it's known that data from sensors in the same network are spatially correlated, and considering topology information and the spatial relation of the sensors is essential for accurate leak detection. 1.2. Study Objectives Recent research progress has demonstrated the effectiveness of DNNs in solving LD&L problems, and it is becoming increasingly clear that DNNs present an opportunity for improving current practice. In this work, we will be leveraging some of the most recent advances in DNNs, to develop a robust methodology for achieving accurate real-time LD&L in complex settings. Our\r\np.7: The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 presents the background of semi-supervised anomaly detection (SSAD) and CDCGANs. Section 3 presents the proposed methodology for LD&L. Section 4 details the application of the method to a WDN, followed by a discussion of the main findings. Finally, Section 5 draws the main conclusions of the work and introduces some potential extensions. 2. Theoretical Background 2.1. Semi-Supervised Deep Anomaly Detection Leaks result in anomalous pressure observations, and most notably, a contextual anomaly. Therefore, LD&L in WDNs can be described as a particular case of the general problem of anomaly detection and isolation in dynamic systems (Soldevila et al., 2016). Most problems in this context suffer from the limited availability of labeled anomalous data (Schlegl et al., 2019). For LD&L, this is mostly because (a) historical monitoring data on leak events is often scarce and may be unreliable, (b) full-scale physical experiments (using e.g. fire hydrants) are very costly, and (c)\r\np.8: discriminator (𝐷) neural network (Goodfellow et al., 2014). A random input latent space 𝑧~𝑝𝑧 (often sampled from a normal or uniform probability distribution, e.g. 𝑧~𝒩(0,1)) is mapped to the data space 𝜙 by the generator, which tries to generate new examples that are ideally identical to the training dataset 𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎. The discriminator is responsible for classifying a given generator output 𝜙 as either real (i.e. indistinguishable from the training dataset 𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎) or fake (non-identical to the training dataset) (Wang et al., 2017). These models are trained together in a zero-sum manner, also called min-max and adversarial, such that improvements in the discriminator come at the cost of reduced capability of the generator, and vice versa (Arjovsky and Bottou, 2017; Gui et al., 2021). This can be represented by the following objective function (Zheng et al., 2020): min 𝐺max 𝐷 𝔼𝜙~𝑝𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑎[𝑙𝑜𝑔𝐷(𝜙)] + 𝔼𝑧~𝑝𝑧[log⁡(1 −𝐷(𝐺(𝑧)))]\r\np.8: (including very sharp) data distributions (Wunderlich and Sklar, 2022). GANs allow for the synthesis of novel images, videos, numeric and audio data, or text from a random input (Al Qahtani et al., 2021). Hence, GANs are commonly used for data synthesis to facilitate the training of other ML models when data is insufficient or to correct the overtraining of a DNN (Zamora et al., 2021). For image data, the generator and discriminator frequently take the form of deep CNNs (see Radford et al., 2015 as a pioneering work). The resulting architecture is often referred to as deep convolutional GAN (DCGAN). 2.3. Conditional Training GAN models generate new plausible examples of a given dataset, but their outputs are practically random and uncontrollable. There is no way to control the outputs other than discovering the complex relationship between the input latent space and the GAN-generated outputs, which is generally difficult and often unfeasible (Wang et al., 2018). However, GANs can be conditioned on auxiliary inputs, allowing for the targeted generation of outputs. This can be done by hot-\r\np.9: can be used as a standalone model to generate data in the domain of interest. The most common strategy is to condition GANs on class labels (i.e. class-conditional GANs), but GANs can also be conditioned on the auxiliary image(s) in the context of image-to-image translation tasks (Brownlee, 2019). The conditional training of a DCGAN-based model is referred to as CDCGAN. 3. Methodology The proposed methodology employs a CDCGAN in the context of SSAD to identify and locate leaks in a WDN. The methodology is based on four pillars: (1) hydraulic model-based generation of training data, (2) conversion of hydraulic model input-output pairs into images, (3) semi- supervised training of a CDCGAN model for image-to-image translation, and (4) using the structural similarity (SSIM) index for LD&L. These pillars are described in the following subsections. A flowchart of the proposed methodology is provided in Fig. 1.\r\np.10: 3.1. Hydraulic Model-Based Generation of Training Data In a hypothetical leak-free condition, it is subjectively assumed that the uncertain demand in node 𝑖 and time step 𝑡 (denoted by 𝑑𝑖,𝑡) is represented by a normal distribution with a mean (𝜇(𝑑𝑖,𝑡)) equal to the field data (or estimates) of demand, and a standard deviation of⁡𝜎(𝑑𝑖,𝑡) = 0.1𝜇(𝑑𝑖,𝑡) (Soldevila et al., 2017). We employ Latin hypercube sampling (LHS) to produce a variety of random, plausible values for pipe parameters and demand time series and feed them to the EPANET model to obtain the resulting pressure time series in the observation nodes. We refer to data obtained from hydraulic simulations as ‘model-generated data’. 3.2. Transforming Demand and Pressure Data to Images Demand and pressure values at various observation points (OPs) in a single time step are employed\r\np.14: 2020), following the scheme described in sub-sections 3.1 and 3.2. In this context, 365 daily demand patterns with a time step of 5 minutes have been generated, taking into account demand uncertainty. This results in 105,120 pairs of demand-pressure images, which are divided into training and testing data by an 80%-20% split. This procedure is first done using pressure observations in 780 nodes in the WDN (two nodes close to the reservoirs, which have exceptionally high pressures, have been omitted from the original 782 nodes of the case study), and then repeated by only incorporating the 33 locations for which pressure sensors are available (see Fig. 3).\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT P19_Leak_detection_topology_2025.txt\r\np.1: “Telecommunications of the Future” (PE00000001 - program “RESTART”). anomalies [6], [7]. However, their applicability and effec- tiveness are limited by the high computational cost and the need for large labeled datasets for training, which are not always available in the context of WDNs. In contrast, model- based approaches have shown promising results in identify- ing network anomalies by leveraging physical principles and mathematical models to estimate water flow and pressure [8]. These methods, however, often lack the capability to precisely localize leaks within the network, which is a critical limitation for effective usability in WDNs. In this paper, we introduce a novel dynamic model for WDNs based on Topological Signal Processing (TSP) a tool\r\np.1: the capability of static approaches by incorporating temporal dynamics into the analysis, allowing for the characterization of transient water demands and persistent leakages. In addition, cost limitations and the inaccessibility of certain locations often restrict the number and placement of sensors, preventing the widespread deployment of IoT devices. Therefore, we reconstruct the edge signals from a subset of observed samples and we formulate an optimization problem to detect water leakages. IV. CONCLUSIONS In this paper, we introduced a novel dynamic topology- based learning approach for leak detection in Water Distribu- tion Networks. By leveraging Topological Signal Processing, we modeled water flow dynamics using high-order representa- tions that extend the traditional graph-based frameworks. This approach allowed us to efficiently reconstruct flow signals from a limited number of sensor measurements of edge flows and accurately identifying leakages within the network. GitHub repository, accessed on March 13, 2025.\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT P25_Encoder_decoder_2024.txt\r\np.1: autoencoders (AEs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), and long short-term memory autoencoders (LSTM-AEs). The multivariate pressure sig- nals reconstructed from these models are analysed for leakage identification. The leak onset time is estimated using a fast approximation sliding window technique, which computes statistical discrepancies in prediction errors. The performance of all three variants is validated using the widely studied L-Town benchmark network. Furthermore, their feasibility for real-world application is studied by applying them to a real-world case study representing the data availability and network design often found in smaller- and medium-sized utilities in Norway. The results for the benchmark network showed that AE and LSTM-AE showed comparable detection performance for abrupt leaks with VAE performing the least. For incipient leaks, the LSTM-AE showed better detection performance with few false-positives. For the real-world dataset, the performance was significantly lower due to the quantity and quality of data available, and the contradiction of inherent requirements of data-driven models. In addition, the analysis revealed that the positioning of pressure sensors in the network is critical for the leak detection performance of these models. Key words: anomaly detection, autoencoders, deep neural networks, multivariate timeseries HIGHLIGHTS • Three variants of encoder–decoder neural networks are modelled for data-driven leak detection.\r\np.2: Leak Identification and Localisation Algorithm LSTM-AE long short-term memory autoencoder MAE mean absolute error MAPE mean absolute percentage error MSE mean squared error PMA pressure measurement area RNN recurrent neural network\r\np.3: either a transient based model or a steady state model based on extended period analysis. The transient model determines the magnitude and location of leaks by observing changes in the pressure and flow signals due to the presence of leaks in the network (Colombo et al. 2009; Che et al. 2022). Transient models, while predicting the location and magnitude of leaks with high accuracy, are often constrained to smaller networks due to modelling complexities and scalabilities. Steady state approaches, on the other hand, are based on inverse transient analysis for determining leak magnitude and location (Pudar & Liggett 1992; Kapelan et al. 2003). Though these classes of model-based methods were able to detect leaks accu- rately, even for larger networks, they require complete information on the network hydraulics and continuous calibration which is seldom available for smaller utilities. In recent years, due to the advancements in measuring systems and increasing computational power, many water utilities are moving towards digital solutions for real-time monitoring and control of WDNs (Cominola et al. 2015; Mounce 2021), monitoring pressures, flows and the performance and settings of pumps and valves. The often-observed decision of utilities to focus on pressure sensors is mostly based on the fact that they are more cost efficient and easier to install compared to flow meters which are usually expensive and require intensive installation procedures.\r\np.3: Classification-based approaches were also widely used for leakage detection in the last decade (Cody et al. 2017; Soldevila et al. 2017; Romero et al. 2021, 2022). These methods are based on Support Vector Machines (e.g., Cody et al. 2017), Baye- sian classification (e.g., Soldevila et al. 2017), K-Nearest Neighbour clustering (e.g., Soldevila et al. 2016) and many other similar strategies. These methods assume that correctly labelled leak events are available during the model training process. However, in practice, it is difficult to obtain such labelled data for all types of possible leak events in WDNs. Hence, the pre- dictive performance for these models is limited to leak events that are similar to labelled historical events. The third sub-class of data-driven methods, namely, prediction methods is the most widely used for leakage detection. A prediction model is trained based on no-leak data to predict the expected values for no-leak scenarios. If a leak event occurs, there will be deviations between model predictions and the measured values. The prediction errors are then attributed to leak events or sensor faults based on the obtained residuals. The residual error analysis is then carried out based on differ- ent evaluation metrics such as mean squared error (MSE), mean absolute error (MAE), and mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) for determining the model performance and onset of leak events (Wan et al. 2022). Prediction-based artificial neural network (ANN) models have been used widely for leak detection in WDNs (Mounce et al. 2010, 2014; Cody et al. 2020;\r\np.4: • Measurements are available for nominal system states that represent no-leak scenarios over a sufficient period of time. If they are not available, pressure estimates at the corresponding sensor locations are available from a calibrated hydraulic model. • Data quality for the measurements is high enough to allow for training and application of the algorithms, or at least for adequate data preprocessing. The main objective of the study is to evaluate the applicability of data-driven methods under these widely used assumptions on a real-world case study network in Norway. We highlight that the number and location of the pressure sensors and data quality play an important role in realising these methods in everyday scenarios. In this study, three data-driven prediction models are trained using only pressure signals, and their leak detection perform- ance is compared using a widely studied leak detection benchmark, which fulfils the aforementioned assumptions. The three deep neural network models – autoencoders (AEs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), and long short-term memory autoenco- ders (LSTM-AEs) – are trained in semi-supervised fashion using only pressure measurements. To explore their applicability in practice, the models were also trained and evaluated using measurements from a representative real-world WDN in Norway.\r\np.4: ology section. The results and discussion on the detection performance of the three models used and practical limitations are then provided. Finally, the conclusions and future directions of the study are presented. METHODOLOGY The methodology in this study comprises three stages. In stage 1, the training data are obtained from pressure measurements during nominal system states representing (assumed) no-leak scenarios or from estimates using a hydraulic model calibrated for nominal operations. The training data are then pre-processed for subsequent neural network training and evaluation. As the availability of historical leak events for a given WDN is rarely available, semi-supervised learning based deep neural net- works are used for leakage detection in this study. In this regard, in stage 2, three classes of encoder–decoder deep neural networks (AE, VAE, and LSTM-AE) are trained in a semi-supervised fashion. The encoder–decoder models are selected in an increasing level of model complexity and their performance for different types of leaks is compared. Subsequently, in stage 3, the predictions and real measurements are used for detecting the time of leak using statistical moving window tech- niques. Figure 1 illustrates the proposed methodology. Figure 2 shows the difference in architecture for the three types of models described in this section. In summary, the three\r\np.5: vations at hidden layers. The compressed data at latent layer z capture the most relevant features of the original data. The decoding network (D) then takes the compressed data z as input and transforms it back to the original space Rm, by increasing the dimensionality at each of the hidden layers. The output of AE ^x can be represented as ^x ¼ D(z) where z ¼ E(x). During training, AEs aim to minimise the difference between the input data (x) and the reconstructed data ^x. MSE as a loss function, is used to train the AE model. By minimising this error, the AE learns a compressed representation that captures the most salient features of the input data. Hence, any considerable deviations in the reconstruction errors can be attributed to the onset of anomalous events. AEs have been widely used in classification and regression tasks across several domains. For example, AEs have been used for monitoring pipeline leakage (Wang et al. 2020) and for monitoring spacecraft sensor data (Sakurada & Yairi 2014). In this study, a simple AE is trained in semi-supervised fashion for the leak detection task. Long short-term memory autoencoder LSTM are a type of recurrent neural networks (RNNs) that are used for learning patterns in the data. LSTMs have been widely popular and are building blocks of advanced sequence learning architectures. They are widely used for language trans-\r\np.5: decoder in an LSTM-AEr reconstructs the sequential data from the compressed representation. Figure 2(b) shows an illustra- tive LSTM-AE architecture. It uses LSTM layers that process the latent representation in reverse order, effectively generating a sequence of outputs. Similar to traditional AEs, LSTM-AEs aim to minimise the difference between the input sequence and the reconstructed sequence. The training process involves propagating the reconstruction error backwards through the LSTM layers and adjusting the model’s weights to improve the reconstruction accuracy. Like AE, LSTM-AE were also widely popular across multiple domains. They were initially illustrated for video represen- tation learning (Srivastava et al. 2015), and air quality monitoring (Wei et al. 2022) and found applications in oil and gas pipeline leakage monitoring (Spandonidis et al. 2022). Variational autoencoder VAEs introduce probabilistic modelling into the AE framework, enabling the generation of new data samples based on learned probability distributions. VAE replace reconstruction errors with reconstruction probability through Bayesian Figure 2 | Three class of encoder–decoder model used for leak detection: (a) AE, (b) LSTM-AE, and (c) VAE. Water Supply Vol 00 No 0, 5\r\np.6: inference during the training process (Kingma & Welling 2014). Like AE, VAE constitutes encoder (E) and decoder (D) but varies by inferential learning at the latent space z. Specifically, the latent vector learns two lower-dimensional vectors: mean (μ) and variance (σ) vectors that parameterise a multivariate normal distribution (refer Figure 2(c)). These are used for infer- ential learning of the encoder and decoder parameters. The model prediction ^x is done through a reparameterisation trick, where the compressed information at latent space z is sampled via inferential learning and fed to the decoder. The decoder then reconstructs the sampled information at latent space to the original dimension of input data x. VAEs aim to minimise the reconstruction error, similar to other AEs. Additionally, they also optimise the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between the learned distribution and the standard normal distribution (prior), ensuring that the latent space distribution remains close to a unit Gaussian. With the inferential learning abilities, VAEs can also be used as a generative model. For example, to generate synthetic data\r\n"
