INTRODUCTION Water Distribution Networks (WDNs) are critical infrastruc- ture systems that supply clean water to residential, commer-\r\np.1: Furthermore, no prior work has systematically evaluated the impact of different demand patterns on model performance or compared the predictive capabilities of MLPs and CNNs under varying topological conditions and data availability. In this paper, we address these gaps by proposing a novel deep learning-based approach for pressure prediction in WDNs that combines standard hydraulic parameters with Topological Metrics (TMs) to enhance predictive accuracy and robustness. The key innovation lies in the feature selection strategy rather than the model architecture itself, with a specific focus on node centrality information. Node centrality quantifies the structural importance of a node within a network based on its connectivity and positional influence, measuring its con-\r\np.1: The proposed workflow involves (i) selecting the WDN topology, (ii) generating datasets through simulation, (iii) eval- uating node centrality, (iv) identifying an optimal feature set for training pressure-prediction models, and (v) evaluating and comparing the performance of MLPs and CNNs across varying demand patterns and topological scenarios. We validate the ap- proach using four well-known benchmark networks (Fossolo, IFIP Networking 2025 - Limassol, Cyprus - 26-30 May 2025 ISBN 978-3-903176-72-0 © 2025 IFIP\r\np.2: were simulated over periods of 300 days, capturing variations from typical daily cycles to stochastic week-long patterns. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves high predictive accuracy (up to R2 = 0.9999) and re- mains effective across different demand and centrality patterns. Comparative analysis highlights the advantages of MLPs over CNNs under limited topological information, underscoring the effectiveness of the streamlined feature-selection strategy. The key contributions of this work are: • A novel feature selection strategy combining hydraulic parameters and centrality metrics for pressure prediction in WDNs. • A deep learning-based methodology that is inherently\r\np.2: community [7]. Several studies have addressed the challenge of modelling and predicting pressure variations in Water Distribution System (WDS) nodes, using ML techniques to improve the accuracy of simulations and the effectiveness of water network management [7], [13]. Supervised learning approaches using Support Vector Machine (SVM) [14] and Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) [15] have been applied to de- tect pressure abnormalities. More recent studies have explored deep learning techniques, including CNNs [11] and RNNs [10], to improve leak detection accuracy. A significant example is Ashraf et al. [16], who developed a deep learning model using GNNs to overcome the limitations of traditional hydraulic simulators. Their approach exploits\r\np.2: hydraulic laws to make state estimates based on minimal data, demonstrating superior effectiveness compared to conventional methods, especially in large networks. In another study [17], the authors address the challenge of forecasting multivariate geo-sensor time series, focusing on the complex spatial and temporal correlations present in WDSs. They propose a hybrid analysis method that integrates both spatial and temporal cor- relations. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach outperforms existing methods in terms of accuracy for predicting flow and pressure within WDSs. Techniques such as Gradient Correction Gated Recurrent Unit (gcGRU) and image-based deep learning have been used in studies for pressure prediction [18], [19]. For example, Kan\r\np.2: burst detection method for water networks using a prediction- classification approach. A Support Vector Regressor (SVR) model predicts pressure values, and a decision tree identifies bursts by analyzing multi-time-step data with statistical con- trol rules. The method combines multiple features (absolute pressure, prediction deviation, pressure variation) to achieve a detection accuracy of 99.56%, with prediction deviation being the most significant factor. By using data from consecutive moments and dynamic thresholds, it improves burst detection within 15 minutes. These works demonstrate how integrating ML into pressure management at WDS nodes can not only improve leakage detection, but also optimize the planning and operation of\r\np.2: in the literature employs an approach similar to ours, where models trained on different water demand patterns are com- pared. Furthermore, node centrality information is leveraged to achieve more reliable and robust pressure forecasts. III. Conclusions This article presents a review of the main research items in the steady-state software-based leak detection/localization field. An intro- duction about the motivation behind the study of these methods is provided, and the main characteristics of water distribution networks, DMAs and the posed leak management problems are described. A complete review of the majority of existing methodologies is provided, including a categorization into the main families: model-based, mixed model-based/data-driven and data-driven. A discussion from the point of view of the interests of water companies is presented and a compara-\r\np.23: tive performance analysis of a model-based and a data-driven approach is developed, based on the recent and well-known benchmark from the BattLeDIM2020 competition. This study allowed to derive some conclusions about the implementation, advantages and limitations of a representative of each family. About the classification of the reviewed works, a trend can be found in the evolution of the leak detection/localization strategies: from model-based schemes that typically require the hydraulic model of the network, the development of machine learning and data mining algorithms motivated the interest on mixed model-based/data-driven strategies, which learn the behaviour of the network from different leak scenarios. Leak detection and localization Problem statement. An essential focus of optimizing and digitizing WDN\r\np.11: WDNs. Leaks are identified as deviations from expected behavior by comparing simulated data (e.g., pressure or flow) to real-time measure- ments. While effective in controlled environments, these methods suffer from several limitations: • They require extensive domain knowledge and substantial com- putational resources to build and calibrate hydraulic models. • Uncertainties in model parameters, such as pipe conditions and demand patterns, reduce accuracy. • Changes in network topology demand frequent recalibration, lim- iting scalability and adaptability to real-world, dynamic scenar- ios. These limitations have spurred the rise of data-driven methods, which bypass the need for detailed hydraulic models by directly an-\r\np.11: Beyond purely data-driven approaches, hybrid methods integrate data-driven leak detection with model-based leak localization, lever- aging sensor data patterns and hydraulic simulations for improved accuracy. For example, Daniel et al. [120] follow a hybrid approach by identifying leaks using semi-supervised linear regression on pres- sure data and refining leak localization through a simulation-based optimization framework. Journal of Industrial Information Integration 47 (2025) 100880\r\np.12: localize them by leveraging the topological information embedded in the GNN models. Zhang et al. [97] highlight that data-driven methods often struggle to generalize to out-of-distribution data, and to address this limitation, they propose an algorithm-informed graph neural net- work (AIGNN) for leakage detection and localization in WDNs, inspired by the perfect generalization ability of classical algorithms. Building on Gardarsson’s work [43], AIGNN is trained to emulate the Ford- Fulkerson algorithm for max-flow problems, with two models deployed to reconstruct and predict pressures based on current and previous measurements, respectively, detecting leaks by analyzing discrepancies between the outputs. Case study: GNN for leak detection [86]. In this work, Zanfei et al. pro- pose a classification model to identify bursts from abnormal data in a\r\np.12: temporal dependencies. The final output is generated through a dense layer with a threshold for binary classification. For both models, the threshold for generating binary output was chosen to maximize detection rates while minimizing false positives on the training dataset. The methods were tested on the Modena network (Section 5). The data for the study was generated following the procedure described by Menapace et al. [122], simulating water de- mands with deterministic trends and normally distributed randomness. Burst events were modeled with a Poisson distribution, representing pipe crack types such as longitudinal, circumferential, and spiral. The flow rate time series were collected from the outflows of tanks and reservoirs, while pressure time series were retrieved from selected monitoring points. The selection of these points follows the method\r\n\r\nDOCUMENT P27_Benchmark_protocol_review_2026.txt\r\np.21: Water 2026, 18, 2267 21 of 30 cross-scenario validation, cross-network validation, and synthetic-to-real transfer. Such a framework would provide a more realistic assessment of model robustness and facilitate comparison between studies. Digital twin environments [97] and com- bined benchmark–field evaluation frameworks [10] provide promising platforms for implementing these evaluation strategies. 3. Adoption of operationally meaningful evaluation metrics: Conventional classification metrics should be complemented by evaluation measures that reflect operational decision-making. The economically weighted scoring framework introduced in the BattLeDIM competition [19], which jointly considers detection timeliness and localiza-\r\np.21: making and quantified prediction uncertainty. Therefore, future studies should report uncertainty estimates, robustness analyses, and model interpretability using standard- ized procedures to improve confidence in operational applications [26,37,90–93]. Table 5. Proposed minimum reporting checklist for ML/DL water leak detection studies. Dimension Required Reporting Items Dataset provenance Dataset category (public benchmark, bespoke synthetic, laboratory, or field); network topology and size; simulation software and version (for synthetic datasets); utility anonymization procedure (for field datasets). Leak scenario characterization Leak type (abrupt, incipient, background, single, or concurrent); leak magnitude range (absolute value and percentage of system demand); leak onset generation\r\np.21: Sensing modality; number and location of sensors; sampling frequency; sensor specifications (field/laboratory datasets) or noise model (synthetic datasets). Data partitioning Training, validation, and testing strategy (random, leave-scenario-out, or temporal); measures to prevent information leakage; class distribution within each partition. Evaluation metrics Definition of all reported metrics; detection delay; localization metric (node-based, top-k, or distance-based); false alarm rate and evaluation period. Baseline comparison Comparison with one or more public benchmark datasets (e.g., LeakDB or BattLeDIM/L-Town), where applicable; baseline implementation details and hyperparameter settings. For modalities without an established public benchmark, such as acoustic leak detection at the time of writing, comparison against a\r\np.21: demand uncertainty, and sensor failures; uncertainty quantification, where applicable. Reproducibility Data availability statement; repository link or documented access restrictions; source code availability; software environment and implementation specifications. The proposed reporting framework and accompanying recommendations provide a practical foundation for improving transparency, reproducibility, and comparability, but their feasibility has not yet been empirically validated. Accordingly, Table 5 is presented as a proposal for prospective testing rather than as an established standard. Adoption may be constrained by journal page limits, data-sharing policies, the absence of supplementary- material mechanisms in some venues, and disincentives to report validation weaknesses or uncertain estimates. A tiered implementation is therefore proposed: dataset category, validation scheme, test-set independence, class balance, and the primary metric should be\r\np.22: should be regarded as a transparent minimum proposal requiring empirical evaluation and refinement. 8. Discussion and Future Directions 8.1. Principal Findings and Limitations Five major findings emerge from this review. First, the reuse of standardized bench- marks remains limited and represents an exception rather than an established practice. Although benchmarking terminology is reported in 36 studies, the detailed extraction re- veals that only 12 studies employed the BattLeDIM or L-Town dataset, 7 relied on LeakDB, and 4 used the Aghashahi acoustic dataset. In several cases, benchmark usage was at- tributed to the reuse of a common network topology, such as the Hanoi network, while the associated leak scenarios, data generation procedures, and evaluation protocols were independently defined by the authors. The first attempt to establish a unified bench- mark framework enabling comparison across multiple architectures was only reported in\r\np.22: generated laboratory experiments or field measurements. This separation limits direct com- parison between methodologies and constrains the assessment of generalization capability across sensing technologies. Third, validation methodology represents the least consistently reported aspect among the reviewed studies. Among the 90 analyzed publications, only 7 applied cross-validation procedures and 9 adopted temporal or independent hold-out validation strategies. In contrast, 32 studies relied on random data splitting, while 42 either did not report the validation procedure or provided insufficient details for its reconstruction. This issue is particularly critical because random splitting of pooled simulated scenarios may introduce information leakage, leading to overestimated model performance when similar network conditions or leak characteristics are simultaneously present in training and testing datasets. Fourth, progress toward standardized evaluation practices has not followed a continu- ous upward trend. The proportion of studies using shared datasets reached its maximum\r\np.22: value during 2022–2023, corresponding to the period influenced by the BattLeDIM com- petition, where 45.0% of studies adopted common datasets. However, this proportion decreased to 22.6% during 2024–2026. A similar trend was observed for more rigorous validation approaches: the combined use of cross-validation and temporal or hold-out strategies increased from 12.5% to 25.0% and subsequently declined to 16.1% (Figure 6). These observations suggest that benchmark adoption was temporarily strengthened by external initiatives but was not maintained as the research field expanded. Several factors plausibly contribute to this decline, although the reviewed studies do not directly inves- tigate the cause, and these should be read as interpretation rather than established fact. A dilution effect is one candidate: the rapid expansion of the field after 2023 (Section 3.1) brought in a larger number of newcomer research groups and venues, which may be less https://doi.org/10.3390/w18182267\r\np.23: Water 2026, 18, 2267 23 of 30 likely to have engaged with the BattLeDIM or LeakDB benchmarks specifically. Publication pressure in a fast-moving, competitive area may also favor rapid demonstration of a new architecture over the additional effort of preparing and reporting a rigorous validation or benchmark comparison. If the decline continues, the practical implication is that reviewers and venues, rather than the benchmarks themselves, may need to play a larger role in en- forcing standardized evaluation, since voluntary adoption appears not to be self-sustaining. Consequently, improvements in evaluation practice appear to depend strongly on sustained community-driven benchmarking efforts rather than isolated competitions. Fifth, variation in evaluation procedures should be regarded as a fundamental method- ological concern rather than a minor reporting inconsistency. The reliability of comparative\r\np.23: As shown in Figure 7, a headline accuracy of at least 95% was reported in 62.5% of the applicable studies, and the highest group median was obtained for laboratory datasets. By contrast, a true positive rate of 65.22% for previously unseen leaks was reported in the only blind evaluation identified in the review. Because accuracy and true positive rate are different metrics and the underlying tasks also differ, these values cannot be interpreted as a direct performance gap. Nevertheless, the contrast demonstrates that model claims cannot be separated from dataset design, validation, and metric selection. Four limitations of this review are acknowledged. First, a single database was used (Scopus). Although Scopus provides broad coverage of the peer-reviewed water- engineering and computer-science literature relevant to this interdisciplinary topic, com- plementary searches of other databases could enlarge the evidence base. Second, the corpus-level thematic quantification in Section 3 is based on title, abstract, and keyword\r\np.23: 2026 are included whose citation impact and eventual influence cannot yet be assessed. Fourth, no formal risk-of-bias or methodological quality assessment was performed on the included studies; the review instead reports what each study discloses about its data, validation, and evaluation practices, without independently judging the soundness of the underlying research. 8.2. Future Research Directions Beyond the standardization recommendations discussed in Section 7, several research priorities are identified from the findings of this review. Future investigations should include: (i) systematic evaluations of the impact of sce- nario leakage resulting from random splitting strategies applied to simulated datasets; (ii) benchmark frameworks in which sensor placement and leak detection performance are optimized jointly rather than evaluated under predefined sensing configurations [99]; (iii) transfer learning and domain adaptation benchmarks covering multiple utilities, sup-\r\np.24: Water 2026, 18, 2267 24 of 30 artificial intelligence-based leak detection approaches from experimental validation toward practical implementation in operational water distribution systems. 9. Conclusions Substantial advances have been made in water-leak detection through machine learn- ing and deep learning; however, the methodological and evidentiary foundations have not progressed at the same rate as algorithmic innovation. In this systematic review, 102 Scopus- indexed records were examined, of which 90 studies met the inclusion criteria. Limited standardization of datasets, validation procedures, and reporting practices was identified. Bespoke synthetic datasets were used in approximately 40% of the included studies, and a similar proportion was based on private laboratory or utility measurements.
